### Page 10

TO P I C  4 | EL EC TR I F I C ATI O N
Türkiye is championing a voluntary pledge to lift electrification from 23% to 35% by 2035
Electrification is expected to be at the centre of this “implementation COP”, targeting a rise in electricity’s share of global final energy use from 23% to 35% by 2035. We expect support for this
action given it is being positioned alongside energy security, but we also expect discussion and debate over how it is delivered (e.g., the role of renewables, nuclear and natural gas), alongside

concerns over clean tech dependency in supply chains. China and the EU have expressed support but not yet committed.
Some may brand this the electrification COP
The Action Agenda pledge aims to raise electricity's share of global final energy from 23%
today to 35% by 2035. The pledge was launched in Bonn this past June and formalised as the
"35-by-35 Global Electrification Pledge" in New York on 21 September.
China and the EU have signaled support, but have not yet signed the pledge

Electrification fits modern day energy-security goals making it a viable target for this year’s
COP. China’s cleantech dominance means it has a further incentive to support initiatives that
seek to increase the pace of global electrification. It also aligns closely with China's existing
development trajectory. While the 35% goal is based on Paris-aligned pathways supported by
IEA and IRENA analysis, the numerical target itself does not require the additional electricity to

come from non fossil fuel sources. This has invited some criticism, though it may make it
easier for some nations to sign the non-binding pledge.
Alignment with the voluntary renewables pledge
Recall that COP28 called for tripling global renewable power capacity by 2030 in COP28’s
formal Global Stocktake decision, equivalent to around 11.2 TW. IRENA’s September 2026
assessment puts capacity at 5.15 TW at end-2025. Record additions of 693 GW in 2025 remain

below the 1,200 GW needed annually in 2026–30. The 35-by-35 pledge complements this
supply goal by shifting transport, buildings and industry towards electricity, enabling
renewable power to replace fossil fuels. Its emissions benefit still depends on cleaner
generation.
We view the goal positively, but delivery needs grid enhancements.
IRENA counts ~2,500 GW of renewable, storage and large-load projects awaiting connection
and says annual investment to strengthen grid infrastructure and enhance system flexibility

must rise from ~$500bn in 2025 to around $1tn through 2035, and $1.2tn thereafter. That
said, signatories signal support, but domestic delivery plans will determine investment.
Sources: UNFCCC, COP31 Presidency, IEA, IRENA

TO P I C  5   |   F O S S I L  F U EL S
The fossil fuel transition roadmap track is growing outside the formal COP process
COP30 failed to agree a fossil fuel transition roadmap, despite support from more than 80 countries. Brazil’s COP30 presidency is therefore developing a roadmap outside formal UN
negotiations to be presented ahead of COP31. Separately, Colombia and the Netherlands convened a ‘coalition of the willing’ of 57 countries in Santa Marta in April 2026 to explore practical

steps towards a fossil fuel phase-out. This parallel forum allows willing countries to cooperate without needing consensus from all COP parties.
Sources: Carbon Brief, Santa Marta conference report

TO P I C  6 | TR AD E
Trade could resurface at COP31 as a challenge to collective agreement; talks have been requested
Trade tensions could feature at COP31 as governments weigh climate ambition against industrial competitiveness. Developing countries fear that measures such as the CBAM disadvantage

their exporters, while the EU says they prevent carbon leakage. COP30 agreed three annual dialogues on trade's role in climate cooperation (no binding outcome), creating for the first time a
formal space for trade discussions in the UNFCCC process, but The African Group and others would like it on the formal COP31 agenda.
Do trade discussions belong at COP?
Developing countries have long pushed back against climate-related trade measures such as
the EU's Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM) and Deforestation Regulation (EUDR).

They argue such measures are protectionism disguised as climate policy and
disproportionately impact poorer countries. Developed countries view such measures as
necessary to level the playing field for domestic firms in cases where foreign competitors face
lower environment-related compliance costs relative to domestic industries, while also arguing
that COP is not the appropriate forum to discuss trade matters. Despite this, a European
Parliament briefing noted trade is a point of “exposure” for the EU at COP31.

COP30’s agreement on trade dialogue was notable, but it may not be enough
COP30 agreed three annual climate-trade dialogues at the mid-year sessions in 2026-28, with
a high-level event in 2028. The first, in Bonn (13 June), was calmer than expected but exposed
the divide: developing countries argued that climate-linked trade measures such as CBAM
raise costs and restrict market access and called for impact assessments within the UNFCCC,
while the EU defended them as responses to environmental externalities. With trade also

raised in other Bonn rooms, it is far from clear the dialogue will contain the issue in Antalya.
The African Group of negotiators has requested a Trade dialogue on the formal agenda.
China has already said it intends to use COP31 to oppose cleantech trade barriers, with its vice
minister of the Ministry of Ecology and Environment saying the event should discuss “in depth”
the “new problem of unilateralism and protectionism undermining global collective efforts” on

climate. The African Group is also pushing for trade to be on the formal agenda. Ultimately,
trade talks could prove more consequential at COP31 than in previous years given inflationary
pressures and concerns over business competitiveness, but it is hard to assess the potential
impact other than possibly causing delays in agreement. However, even if the impact is
limited, the issue could shape negotiations elsewhere outside of the COP process.
Sources: European Parliament, Carbon Brief, Reuters

### Page 13

TOP I C 7 | G EO P O L I TI CS  & MU L TI L ATER AL I S M (1  o f 2 )
Multilateralism is at a crossroads
Geopolitical fragmentation and the US exit from the Paris Agreement make consensus among nearly 200 nations harder, encouraging “coalitions of the willing” (outside of the formal COP
negotiations) to pursue more ambitious climate goals. COP31’s success or failure in securing multilateral agreement or credible voluntary pledges could shape future COPs by showing which

approaches deliver progress. The US retreat from the Paris Agreement will also continue to have far-reaching implications for climate finance. Overall, the EU and China will be expected to lead.
The consensus agreement principle under strain
The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) provides the
multilateral framework for COP, the forum where countries negotiate collective climate
decisions. The process is anchored by the view that all countries must act on climate change,

but the extent of their efforts depends on national circumstances. This puts the onus on
developed countries to lead. The question is whether this principle still holds against a more
fragmented backdrop and transactional diplomatic context as national budgets prioritize
security and business competitiveness. With the COP process still separating developed and
developing countries by their 1992 UNFCCC classifications, developed countries are continuing

to push for wealthier developing nations such as China and Saudi Arabia to play a greater role
on finance. This has been a major point of contention at recent summits.
The US stepping back has far-reaching implications
The US skipped COP30 and left the Paris Agreement. It will formally exit the UNFCCC in
February 2027. This retreat has weighed on broader climate-finance decisions, both from a
monetary sense and through the loss of negotiating power. While cities, states and businesses

from the US continue to support COP efforts (e.g., groups like America Is All In), the federal
administration’s absence has put more pressure on the EU and China to lead.
What does an evolving process look like?
Because formal COP decisions require consensus (or lack of objection), progress often stalls or
moves slowly. COP30 saw Brazil move items where there was strong, but not unanimous,
support such as the fossil fuel roadmap, to separate streams outside the formal COP process.

Countries disappointed with the lack of stronger mitigation action also launched a separate
summit on transitioning away from fossil fuels, held this past April in Colombia. While COP is
the flagship global forum, these actions demonstrate a shift towards “coalitions of the willing”
for issues where global agreement is not possible.
Sources: EU Council, EU Parliament, Carbon Brief, UN, WRI

TO P I C  7 | G EO P O L I TI C S  &  MU L TI L ATER AL I S M (2  o f 2 )
Much will rest on the approach of the EU and China

With the US absent, the EU and China are the two actors with the greatest diplomatic and economic leverage with which to broker compromise agreements between the different factions at
COP31. This will require careful diplomacy, with their interests not fully aligned in some areas; discussions around trade and climate finance may be particularly challenging. At the same time,
both have reiterated their continued support of climate action and multilateral decision making; a successful COP outcome would back up these commitments.

### Page 15

TO P I C  8 | AI  &  C L I MATE
The Antalya AI Pledge puts data-centre power demand on the agenda
We expect AI’s climate potential and rising electricity demand to make it a prominent COP31 topic, prompting debate over COPs’ role in data-centre energy use. The Antalya AI Pledge invites
governments and companies to commit voluntarily to sustainable infrastructure and environmental transparency. Its call for zero- and low-emissions energy infrastructure leaves room for

nuclear power, prompting discussion of its role in meeting data-centre demand alongside renewables.
The Antalya AI Pledge seeks to build stakeholder support for sustainable AI buildout
Türkiye is encouraging stakeholders (inc. corporates) to commit to the pledge, asking
signatories to “declare our shared ambition to make AI part of the climate solution. This
means sustainably powered, efficiently designed, transparently measured and available to all”.

Signatories will commit to: 1) Power and build AI infrastructure and hardware sustainably, 2)
Design, manage or procure AI responsibly across its lifecycle, 3) Support assessment and
reporting of the environmental impacts of AI systems, 4) Strengthen financial support and
technical infrastructure for AI for climate action.
The AI for Clean Technologies Initiative seeks to highlight AI climate solutions
The initiative plans to develop AI-enabled pilot projects (e.g. smart energy systems) and then

link the projects with industry partners and investors, aiming to demonstrate AI’s potential to
contribute to sustainability goals. For grids, the IEA sees AI improving forecasting, congestion
management and outage response, helping operators get more from existing lines.
The dialogue here will set the stage for AI discussions at future COPs
While the pledge is voluntary, many of these themes are already beginning to appear in early-
stage regulatory demands, with transparency and disclosure receiving particular focus. There

will be questions over how effective the pledge can be given the US has disengaged from the
COP process, though California could send a strong signal if it commits to the pledge. We will
be particularly focused on how the tech industry responds. Despite the trend of greenhushing,
we expect many to send delegations to Antalya, with AI companies and investors viewing
responsible practices and community engagement as key to avoiding backlash and delays to

AI buildout. Note that responsible AI was also a hot topic at the Principles for Responsible
Investment conference this year in October.
Sources: COP31 Presidency, IEA

### Page 16

TO P I C  9 | N ATU R E &  C AR B O N  MAR K ETS
Nature and carbon markets have key roles to play in supporting climate action
Carbon credit discussions will centre on government policies to boost demand and corporate engagement, with much of the formal UN architecture now in place. If carbon markets can scale,
the benefits would be wide-ranging (e.g. supporting emissions targets and providing finance for developing countries). Credibility and greenwashing concerns remain key risks however; we

expect prices to diverge in favour of high-integrity credits. The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF) now has ~$7.8bn of sponsor capital against a $10bn end-2026 target.
Sources: Tropical Forest Forever Facility, Coalition to Grow Carbon Markets
16
Voluntary carbon market development
Türkiye has stated a focus on carbon markets at COP31, particularly on government actions to
boost demand, highlighting the potential for carbon markets to unlock more than $50bn in

additional annual finance for developing nations. The Coalition to Grow Carbon Markets
(made up of 13 governments) has announced it will unveil a “Policy Playbook” at COP31 to
support market development. While much of Article 6 (carbon credits under UN process) has
moved to delivery, countries will aim to reach agreement on the funding model for Article 6.2
at COP31. Further carbon market progress stands to have multiple benefits; it can support
developed countries in hitting climate goals while reducing immediate decarbonisation

requirements, while also providing finance and supporting ecosystems in developing ones.
Understanding the pushback
Carbon markets have seen pushback over credit integrity (including regulatory scrutiny) and
the risk that offsets delay domestic cuts. UN involvement is needed to enforce common
accounting and integrity rules, such as the Article 6 rulebook finalised at COP29 and due for
review in 2028, that stop the same reduction being claimed twice. Without that backstop,

fragmented bilateral and voluntary schemes risk eroding the trust needed to scale the market.
Update on the TFFF
The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF), launched at COP30 last year, aims for $10bn of
sponsor contributions by year-end. It pays tropical-forest countries per hectare conserved,
discounts payments for each hectare lost or degraded, and directs at least 20% to Indigenous
Peoples and local communities. Its Charter was adopted on 22 July 2026 and received a

tailwind following the re-election of Brazilian President Lula, who is a key supporter. Shortly
after Lula’s re-election, the Netherlands pledged up to $500mn to the fund, bringing the total
pledged to $7.8bn. Further financing commitments are key, with Norway’s $3bn contribution
conditional on the fund reaching at least $10bn by the end of 2026.

TO P I C  1 0 | P AC I F I C , O C EANS  & S EA L EV EL  R I S E
Oceans will be a priority item, with Australia promising to champion Pacific interests
Australia’s ask for ceding the hosting was a Pacific pre-COP, and it has promised to continue championing Pacific interests at COP31. The Taku Pakasoa Declaration, signed at the pre-COP,

reflects Pacific priorities, calling for faster emissions cuts, more adaptation finance and financial system reform. The climate-ocean nexus will also be a focus, including investable solutions.
Championing Pacific interests is a key focus for Australia
As part of the hosting compromise, Australia co-hosted the official pre-COP with Fiji and
Tuvalu (5–8 Oct), attended by countries including Germany, the UAE and India (though some
questioned the low number of international leaders who attended). Australia has pledged to

keep championing Pacific interests as President of the Negotiations.
Taku Pakasoa Declaration reflects Pacific Priorities
Signed on 6 Oct at the pre-COP, it calls for faster emissions cuts, a substantial increase in
climate finance (particularly for adaptation in capacity-constrained countries), reform of the
international financial architecture, and for elevating the ocean-climate nexus. It also
welcomes the 35% electrification goal.
The challenges of rising sea levels are gaining increasing prominence

Sea levels in the region are rising faster than the global average, and half of Pacific Island
infrastructure lies within 500 metres of the coast. The UN General Assembly adopted the UN
Declaration on Sea Level Rise by consensus on 24 Sept; global sea levels rose nearly 6mm in
2024, the highest annual increase on record.
Oceans are key to climate stability, but more finance is needed
Pacific nations emphasise the critical role of oceans as a natural environmental regulator.

They absorb 30% of all carbon dioxide emissions (largest carbon sink) and capture 90% of the
excess heat generated by these emissions. Investor interest in sustainable ocean solutions is
growing, with 2026 YTD blue bond issuance already an annual record, yet bond issuance
supporting UN SDG 14: Life Below Water trails many other categories; scaling this up will be a
focus at COP31. A commitment to delivering “tangible, bankable ocean outcomes” at COP31 is

one of 13 points included as part of the Trabzon Ocean Call to Action unveiled by Türkiye,
while the ORRAA has unveiled a Blue Climate Finance Pathway.
Sources: Taku Pakasoa Declaration (UNFCCC), UN Declaration on Sea Level Rise

F U TU R E O F  C O P
Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, though questions over how COP will evolve are in focus
COP has shifted from setting targets to implementation. Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, where we expect a focus on delivering commitments and climate finance access, reflecting African
priorities. COP33 falls to Asia-Pacific in 2028, with China reportedly considering a bid and South Korea potentially interested. COP31–33 will test delivery in a fragmented world, with a second

Global Stocktake concluding in 2028. Its outcome should inform countries’ next national climate plans (NDCs, their emissions-reduction pledges), due in 2030.

by 2035 and agree to develop
a just transition mechanism.
2026 | COP31: Antalya
Türkiye leads the conference
and Action Agenda while
Australia leads negotiations.
The summit will test whether
pledges can be turned into
delivery and what mechanisms
are most effective. The second
Global Stocktake will begin.
Voluntary ambition
Failure to agree fossil fuel

phaseout steps at COP30 led
Colombia and the Netherlands
to launch the Santa Marta
process, with 57 nations
participating. Brazil’s voluntary
transition roadmap will also be
presented ahead of COP31.
2027
COP32 | Ethiopia
Ethiopia will host COP32 in
Addis Ababa after securing
unanimous African Group
backing.
Delivery and funding access
We expect a focus on
delivering commitments and
improving access to climate
finance, reflecting African
priorities. Adaptation finance,

loss and damage, and
vulnerable countries’ needs
could also see focus.
Stocktake technical
assessment
The technical assessment
begins in June 2027, feeding
evidence into the final political
discussions rather than
constituting the stocktake
outcome.
2028
COP33 | Asia-Pacific
The Asia-Pacific region will
host COP33. India withdrew its
offer in April 2026. There is
potential interest from South
Korea, and China is reportedly
considering a bid.
Second stocktake concludes

The second Global Stocktake
concludes at COP33 in
November 2028. Negotiations
shape the final outcome,
which informs the next round
of national climate plans due
in 2030.
Wider context
A November summit could
coincide with the US elections.
COP reform and the
effectiveness of voluntary
coalitions will remain
important questions.

### Page 19

AP P END I X  |   COP  TER MS
Appendix A: Key COP terms used in this report
This appendix explains the main COP process terms and negotiating concepts used throughout the report. Definitions are summarised for non-specialist readers and reflect how each term is
used in this deck.
Sources: UNFCCC; COP30 and COP31 Presidency documents; and the other sources referenced in this report
19
Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Preview  |  October 2026
TERM DEFINITION

Action Agenda Voluntary, non-binding pledges and initiatives launched alongside the formal talks. It is separate from negotiated decisions.
Annex II / Non-Annex I UNFCCC country classification. Annex II covers developed economies; Non-Annex I covers most other countries, including China and India.
Article 6 Paris Agreement framework for international carbon markets. Article 6.2 covers bilateral trading of carbon credits between countries.
Article 9

Paris Agreement finance provisions. Article 9.1 covers developed
countries’ obligation to provide finance. COP30 set a two-year work
programme for 2026-27.
Baku to Belém Roadmap
to $1.3T
Roadmap to scale climate finance to $1.3tn a year by 2035 from all
sources, inclusive of the $300bn NCQG.
Bonn sessions Mid-year technical talks of the UNFCCC Subsidiary Bodies (e.g. SB64) in Bonn, Germany, which prepare the ground for COP discussions.
Brazil fossil fuel roadmap COP30 Presidency’s voluntary roadmap for transitioning away from fossil fuels, pursued outside the formal UN negotiations.

Consensus How COP decisions are taken: adopted when no party formally objects, so a single party can theoretically block or dilute an outcome.
COP / COPs Conference of the Parties: the UNFCCC’s annual decision-making summit, where nearly 200 parties negotiate climate action.
COP Presidency The host country’s team that chairs the conference. For COP31, Türkiye’s Murat Kurum is COP President and leads the Action Agenda.
TERM DEFINITION
Final COP text Decisions adopted by consensus at the close of the COP, setting its formal outcome.

Formal agenda Negotiated items on the official COP agenda, decided by consensus. Separate from the voluntary Action Agenda.
Global Goal on
Adaptation (GGA)
Paris Agreement goal to strengthen resilience. COP30 adopted 59
indicators; the link to finance is contested.
Global Stocktake (GST) Five-yearly review of progress under the Paris Agreement. The second runs from COP31 to COP33 (2028).
Just transition
mechanism
Belém Action Mechanism agreed at COP30, focused on fairness in the

shift to a low-carbon economy.
Loss and damage fund Fund set up at COP27 and operationalised in 2023 to support vulnerable developing countries.
NCQG New Collective Quantified Goal (COP29): at least $300bn a year for developing countries by 2035, within a $1.3tn ambition.
NDC Nationally determined contribution: each country’s national climate plan, with emissions targets and actions, updated every five years.
Negotiating groups Blocs that coordinate national positions, such as the G77 & China, the EU, LDCs and AOSIS.

Paris Agreement 2015 treaty to hold warming well below 2°C and pursue 1.5°C, with national climate plans (NDCs) as its core mechanism.
Pre-COP Ministerial meeting ahead of the COP to test positions and build consensus. For COP31, Australia co-hosted a Pacific pre-COP.
President of Negotiations COP31 role held by Australia’s Chris Bowen, who drafts final texts and steers government-to-government talks.
UNFCCC UN Framework Convention on Climate Change: the 1992 treaty behind the climate process. Its parties meet annually at the COP.

Appendix C: The level of alarm over global warming has begun to move higher (again)
As part of our monthly Climate & Sustainability Survey, we ask consumers in the UK, US, Germany, France, Spain and Italy for their views on global warming. In Q3 2026, we recorded a notable
rebound in alarm regarding the pace of global warming, particularly in the UK and Europe following a summer of climate extremes. The level of alarm had begun to dip globally post the 2024 US

presidential election in November.

France has also seen its debt rise in relative terms. As recently as the GFC in 2008, France’s
debt-to-GDP was basically in line with Germany’s and well below Italy’s, but today it’s far
above Germany’s and closing in on Italy’s. Outside of the temporary Covid spike, Italian
debt/GDP has been stable for over a decade.

## Document 351: Google cloud beats but rest sucks

### Page 1

From:    陸⾏之  Andrew Lu on global semis and techs <andrewhclu@substack.com>
Subject: Google cloud beats but rest sucks
Date:    19 minutes ago
Google cloud beats but rest sucks
View in browser
Google cloud beats but rest
sucks
⾕歌雲的營收增⻑及資本開⽀回報率都在加速，但營收佔⽐太低無法
⼤幅改善低於預期的舊業務發展，也無法沖銷⾮核⼼業務的⼤幅虧損
ANDREW LU ON GLOBAL SEMI/TECHS
OCT 28 ∙ PAID
READ IN APP
Executive summary
雖然⾕歌雲及⾃研晶⽚ TPUs 的營收，獲利及資本⽀出營收及獲利回報率都在
加速，但因為營收佔⽐太低，加上龐⼤的舊業務不僅營收增速緩慢外⽽且還低
於市場預期，這讓整體營收無法提供投資⼈任何驚喜，外加⼀些⾮核⼼業務虧

損擴⼤將⾕歌雲的獲利貢獻全部消耗殆盡，盤後回檔符合預期。以後要持續關
注佔⽐僅整體營收 1/4 的⾕歌雲發展及⾮核⼼業務虧損是否持續擴⼤，尤其是
關注⾃研晶⽚ TPU 銷售及外租對獲利的影響。
The rest sucks
雖然⾕歌  3Q26 的服務營收仍有 10.6% 的年增，但⽐起  Google Cloud 倍增
的營收明顯不⾜，⽽其中不管是搜尋引擎， Youtube 油管業務都低於預期近
2% ，服務業務營業利潤率也低於預期 1 個百分點，這讓服務業務營業利潤⽐市
場預期低了近 3% 。
除此以外，佔⽐不⼤的其他業務  Other Bets ，營業虧損持續擴⼤，從去年的
同期的 14 億美元營業虧損，擴⼤⾄  3Q26 的 20 億美元的營業虧損（ 19% 虧損
⾼於彭博社分析師預期）；加上⾕歌將發⽣在⺟公司的  AI 研發費⽤獨⽴列表
（其中包括員⼯激勵費⽤，，營業虧損從去年同期的 45 億美元，擴⼤⾄  3Q26

## Document 352: How bad is the rise in bond yields_ (2026-09-23)

Source PDF: all report pdf/How bad is the rise in bond yields_ (2026-09-23).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-23
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

SPECIAL EDITION | HOW BAS IS THE RISE 
IN BOND YIELDS?

TRANSCRIPTION

0:00:10 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Everyone, welcome. As we head into autumn 2026, interest rates are reaching levels never seen for many, many years. 
Why is this happening? Is it sustainable? And what are the implications for the economies of different countries? All these 
questions will be discussed over the next few minutes with the economists of BNP Paribas. Let us begin with Isabelle 
Mateos y Lago, chief economist of the group. Hello, Isabelle.  
0:00:38 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everyone. 
0:00:39 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you for being with us today. Just to begin, a simple explanation. What rates are we talking about and which countries 
are affected? 
0:00:48 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, it is very important to clarify this. So, we are talking about long-term interest rates, rates at 10 years or longer 
maturities, 30 years, etc. The rates at which sovereign states borrow on the market. And this is primarily something that is 
happening in advanced countries, so United States, United King dom, Japan, and then the Eurozone countries. And indeed, 
as you said, we are seeing since the beginning of the summer, interest rates reach levels we had not seen for about 20 
years. But it is important to keep in mind that up until that point, up until the mid -2000s, the level of interest rates that 
we're seeing now were really quite common, quite normal, so one shouldn't overdramatize.  
0:01:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why do we find ourselves in this situation and how did we get to this point?  
0:01:41 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, so the direct cause is that investors are demanding higher interest rates in order to  lend their capital to governments. 
But why is this? Well, there's essentially three reasons for this, and not all of them are bad, and they're not mutually 
exclusive either. The top reason to my mind is that there's just greater competition for capital be tween sovereign states 
and the corporate sector, who are all facing simultaneously additional investment needs, in defense, in artificial 
intelligence, in energy transition, in resilience. And so,  you just have greater competition for capital and that pushes up the 
price of capital, which is the interest rate. 
Reason number two is because markets have revised upwards their expectations of how high central banks are going to be 
taking their interest rates. 
And this mechanically translates into higher rates at the long end. And then there's a third reason, which doesn't apply 
necessarily in every case, but which is essentially higher sovereign risk, a kind of a risk premium, in cases where there are  
concerns arising about the sustainability of these public debts.

### Page 2

2

0:02:59 - Emmanuel Laborde 
What are the implications for global economies? 
0:03:03 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
So that's exactly what we'll be discussing in the next segments. But basically, the two key issues are, one, an adverse 
impact on public finances. It's higher interest rates making it harder to balance the books in terms of public accounts. And 
then secondly, these higher rates tend to slow down economic growth. Of course, by how much and how serious it will be, 
depends on how high interest rates go and how long they stay there. But that's for the next segments.  
0:03:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Yes. Thank you very much, Isabelle. Having said the scene now, let's move on to the first segment with the following 
question. What makes debt sustainable? And let's see what Hélène Baudchon has to say.  
Hello Hélène and welcome into this special edition. 
0:03:58 - Hélène Baudchon 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everybody. 
0:04:01 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When economists talk about public debt, they often refer to the sustainability of debt. As an economist, can you explain to 
us what that means and what is the debt trajectory deemed unsustainable?  
0:04:15 - Hélène Baudchon 
To begin with, I will say that a temporary increase in debt can be seen as a normal evolution, not particularly concerning, 
for instance during an economic crisis or more recently during a health crisis. In such cases, it acts as a buffer. A problem  
arises when the debt level never comes back down. An unsustainable trajectory can be therefore defined, characterized by 
a continuous regular rise in the debt level, both in absolute terms and as a proportion of GDP, driven by high and recurring 
fiscal deficits. And sometimes interest payments can exacerbate the problem.  
0:05:00 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In very simple terms, you are talking about the snowball effect.  
0:05:03 - Hélène Baudchon 
Exactly explaining why consolidation measures are needed in order to prevent such an adverse situation from occurring 
and in order to prevent, in fact, a sudden loss of confidence from creditors.  
0:05:16 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Regarding governments, what should serve as a warning sign for them? And at what point could the situation become, let's 
say, problematic? 
0:05:27 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, simply speaking, when interest rates rise, not so long ago, up until 2022, Eurozone countries were borrowing at very 
low rates and in some instance, even negative rates. And although debt levels were already at high level at that time, the 
low rates helped manage debt servicing. Not much fiscal effort was needed to keep the public debt ratios in check.  
0:05:55 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Of course, we all remember this because it was a very unusual situation, but now things are very different.

### Page 3

3

0:06:01 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed, the situation today is clearly more challenging simply because bond yields are significantly higher and they 
are rising at the time of our discussion. This is for the part the result of the ECB's monetary tightening in 2022 -2023 aimed 
at addressing the inflation shock then. Then long-term interest rates have risen as a consequence and have aligned more 
closely with GDP growth rate. And in technical terms, this is what we refer to as a narrowing of the "r" - "g" spread. 
0:06:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why is this spread so important? Why do you, the economists, monitor it this way?  
0:06:48 - Hélène Baudchon 
Essentially, you have the interest rate "r", which determines the debt burden, while the growth rate "g" determines the 
trajectory of fiscal revenues. And if "r" is lower than "g", and this has been the case for many years, as you can see on the  
chart on the screen, it means that interest payments are increasing at a slower pace than the revenue component of the 
equation. 
0:07:14 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this provides some leeway in other budgetary areas, such as education, of course, defense, maybe pensions.  
0:07:22 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed. And this is what we refer to as the primary budget. However, if and when interest rates do go up while the 
economy isn't growing that much faster, then fiscal leeway diminishes.  
0:07:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Crystal clear. Now, just could you provide a few last words to wrap up the sustainability segment?  
0:07:43 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, to conclude, we are entering a period which demands greater fiscal discipline or, as some might say, less indiscipline 
in order to manage debt trajectories effectively, in particular in the countries which have high debt levels. And for instanc e, 
the Italian case is an interesting one because it is well known for its high debt level ratio. But in fact, it also has an 
advantageous position because it has no primary deficit. In fact, it even benefits from a large primary surplus.  
0:08:22 - Emmanuel Laborde 
As for the countries... For the other countries... I stop you because this will be a discussion with Guillaume Derrien during  
the next segment. Thank you very much, Hélène. 
0:08:30 - Hélène Baudchon 
Thank you, Emmanuel. 
0:08:36 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Hello, Guillaume. Welcome. 
0:08:37 - Guillaume Derrien 
Thank you. 
0:08:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Hello, Emmanuel, and hello to everyone. The flexibility of fiscal policy depends heavily on interest rates, which have 
increased considerably over the past few months. The question is, where do we stand today?

### Page 4

4

0:08:52 - Guillaume Derrien 
So, long-term rates have increased in all advanced economies, reaching levels not seen since 2008 in Europe and in the 
United States, and even since 1996 in Japan. Fortunately, the effective rate that states pay on their debt is lower than thos e 
headline figures suggest, simply because the average maturity of debt is relatively long. Precisely how long are we talking 
about? So, it's about 10 years in Japan, 13 years in the United Kingdom, and 8.5 years in France. That means that most of 
the debt was raised before rates started climbing. 
For example, in France, the effective rate just recently rose above the 2% mark, which is significantly lower than the 10 -
year bond yield, which exceeded 4% for new issuances.  
That said, in the United States, which has an average maturity of roughly six years, is experiencing a more rapid increase in  
its effective rate. 
0:09:54 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this rate increase is happening in the context of high debt -to-GDP ratios and with modest growth, correct? Yes, that's 
correct. 
0:10:05 - Guillaume Derrien 
Debt levels have reached unprecedented heights since the end of World War II. And at the same time, growth is lower, 
hovering around 2% on average in the United States, 1% in Europe and 0.5% in Japan. What does that mean for debt 
servicing costs? So, debt servicing cost is already relatively high, but it is expected to grow further as debt issue before the 
2022 rate hikes mature and are replaced with new instruments bearing higher interest rates. By 2030, interest costs could 
rise to levels last seen in 2000, specifically in France, around 4% of GDP. 
Are all the countries equally affected by these trends? Actually, no. Some countries are managing to reduce their debt 
level. 
Spain, Italy, even Japan have sufficiently improved their primary balance to achieve this, at least for the time being. And o f 
course, we must speak about Germany. Yes, Germany is implementing investment programs that result in a higher public 
deficit and debt-to-GDP ratio. But this ratio started from a relatively low base at around above 60% of GDP. That said, in 
this current context, German bonds have risen. And because German bonds are used as a benchmark for Europe, it  raises 
rates across all Eurozone countries. Is France the only country seeing its public debt go up?  
No, it's not, Emmanuel. This is happening in the United Kingdom and in the United States. These economies, like France, 
are running public deficits that are ever close or exceed 5% of GDP, which is too large for debt ratio to stabilize on its ow n. 
France and the United Kingdom are trying to gradually reduce their public deficit so as not to compromise growth. The 
United States is not going on that path yet, but market pressure could eventually force them to do so.  
0:12:08 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much for your input, Guillaume. Thank you, Emmanuel. Let's go to the next segment. It's about the impact 
of rising bond yields on financing and investments in the Eurozone.  
0:12:26 - Thomas Humblot 
Hello, Thomas. Welcome. Hello. Thank you for having me today.  
0:12:30 - Emmanuel Laborde 
We've just seen what's behind the rise in Eurozone bond yields. This rise is partly attributed to the sovereign spread. What 
impact will this have on the economy? 
0:12:42 - Thomas Humblot 
Actually, this widening of some government bond spreads is obviously not good news, but it tends paradoxically to have 
less impact than an increase in long-term interest rates that does not include the sovereign risk premium. for example, in 
France, for a very long time, it has been considered that the 10 -year OAT was the relevant benchmark for fixed loans. And 
at that time, the spread on French government bonds was relatively stable. But since 2024, that spread increased

### Page 5

5

significantly and that renders the sovereign yield a less relevant benchmark.  
And now the most relevant benchmark is the swap rate because it determines the cost of bank funding that backs new 
loans. 
0:13:56 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Since the end of 2024, we have seen a rise in long-term 10-year swap rates. What impact does this have on bank lending 
rates? 
0:14:08 - Thomas Humblot 
Fixed loans have obviously become more expensive, but to a lesser extent than if they were priced solely based on long -
term sovereign rates. And what about existing fixed-rate loans? 
There is no impact. An increase in rates for the entire maturity of a loan does not have any effect if it is a fixed -rate loan. 
Borrowers with outstanding loans are therefore protected. Obviously, new fixed loans are granted at higher rates. And this 
sharply contrasts with a floating-rates loans. 
Because with floating-rates loans, borrowers experience regular rate adjustments throughout the term of the loan. And in 
terms of bank financing? Strictly in terms of bank financing, an increase in short -term rates has an immediate and 
widespread restrictive impact on borrowers, while for long-term interest rates, the impact is more gradual, and it comes 
gradually as the outstanding amount of fixed-rate loans are progressively renewed. And the thing is that since the 
beginning of the war in Iran, we have seen a combination of the two.  
And in practical terms, the rate of new bank loans to non-financial corporations has increased about 25 basis points in the 
eurozone since the beginning of the year. 
0:16:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Just a few words about companies that borrow directly from the market.  
0:16:16 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, in that case, the impact is even more straightforward. The rate at which a company borrows from the market is the 
risk-free rate plus a premium. 
0:16:28 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Have risk premiums, known as corporate credit spreads, also increased?  
0:16:34 - Thomas Humblot 
Yes, they have increased temporarily at the beginning of the year but have returned to normal since. For the time being, the 
widening remains moderate for the highest-rated investment-grade issuers. 
However, some significant bond issuance in the euro debt market due to some major US technology companies has 
increased spreads for longer maturities. 
0:17:11 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In particular terms, how does this affect the investment decisions of the companies?  
0:17:19 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, investments that were previously worthwhile due to returns sufficient to cover the cost of capital may no longer do so 
and in turn that might lead to a decrease in demand for financing.  
0:17:40 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much Thomas. Now let us move on to the emerging economies. And it is with Cynthia Kalasopatan -Antoine.

### Page 6

6

Hello Cynthia, and welcome. 
0:17:54 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Hi, Emmanuel. Hello, everyone. 
0:17:57 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Long-term bond yields rose significantly in advanced economies over the summer. What can we now say about emerging 
markets? 
0:18:06 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Emerging bond markets held up well, which is quite unusual. The rise in long -term sovereign yields in emerging countries 
has been less pronounced than in advanced economies, including the United States and Germany. Now, there are two 
main exceptions, Turkey and Poland. Meanwhile, we did not witness any significant correction in emerging financial 
markets over the summer, and the main currencies appreciated slightly against the dollar or the euro.  
0:18:37 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Does this mean that public finances are more robust in emerging countries?  
0:18:43 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, it depends. You see, in cases like the Czech Republic and South Korea, public debt is moderate and macroeconomic 
fundamentals are solid. However, these countries are now less frequently considered as emerging and tend to be viewed 
as newly advanced economies. By contrast, Brazil and South Africa have weaker public finances and investors have 
already incorporated a higher sovereign risk into their assessments. And this is reflected in the bond yields. And this 
assessment of sovereign risk did not really change over the summer. 
0:19:26 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When comparing the main fiscal metrics, what are the trends? What do you see as an economist?  
0:19:33 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
On average, the fiscal metrics in emerging markets are not substantially worse than those in advanced economies. The 
average budget deficit levels are similar, ranging from 4% to 5% of GDP in 2025 -2026, according to the IMF. And as in 
advanced economies, the public debt is higher than its pre-Covid level. However, the average public debt to GDP ratio in 
emerging countries excluding China is close to 60%, whereas in advanced economies, it is above 100%.  
0:20:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Do you arrive at the same conclusion when comparing debt sustainability criteria?  
0:20:17 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, there are significant regional differences. In emerging Asia, the factors that determine debt sustainability are more 
often favorable than those in advanced economies. In Central Europe, the gap between the interest rate paid on debt and 
the GDP growth rate is also favorable. However, the primary deficits in some countries are high.  
0:20:45 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In a few words, a brief overview of the situation in Latin America.  
0:20:49 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, Brazil and Mexico are in a less favorable position. These countries would indeed need to generate a significant 
primary budget surplus each year in order to stabilize their debt in the medium term.

### Page 7

7

0:21:07 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Economists use the term original sin, which refers to the situation when governments resort to borrowing in foreign 
currency to address the shortfall in domestic financing. Does this phenomenon continue to distinguish emerging 
countries? 
0:21:24 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Yes, this is still the case. Emerging countries still have a significant share of their debt in foreign currency. This repres ents 
about 35 to 40% of their total debt on average. And in fact, now, this figure has decreased over the past decade, and this 
contributes to their resilience. However, governments in emerging markets have a less favorable position as regards their 
debt profile and their financing costs are also on average higher.  
0:22:02 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And finally, just a few words about a larger subject, China.  
0:22:07 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
China's public debt is significant and growing rapidly. That being said, it is denominated in domestic currency and issued in  
domestic markets. This debt is, however, sustainable in the medium term due to a very low effective interest rate and a 
high growth rate. 
0:22:29 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much, Cynthia, for being with us today.  
0:22:32 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Thank you. 
0:22:33 - Emmanuel Laborde 
It is now time to conclude, and we welcome back Group Chief Economist Isabelle Mateos y Lago.  
It is time for our conclusion with Isabelle. Once again, just a final question, please. Could or should, I do not know which 
word is the good one, central banks intervene to curb this rise in interest rates?  
0:22:58 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Absolutely not. The main contribution that central banks can bring to dealing with this situation is to restore price stabili ty, 
as is their mandate, as promptly as possible. And in the current circumstances, this means keeping relatively high policy 
rates, in some cases, rising them further. And the responsibility for keeping public finances on a sustainable footing rests 
squarely with governments and any attempt by these governments to instead take the easy way out by pressuring central 
banks to lower interest rates prematurely would be enormously counterproductive.  
0:23:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much, Isabelle. Thank you all for joining us today. Just a few words to remind you that additional economic 
research content can be found by scanning the QR code shown on your screen. Do not hesitate to use it with your 
smartphone. It is very easy. It was a pleasure to be with you today. Have a nice day and hope to see you so

WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
SEPTEMBER CPI PREVIEW
Setting the pace for the run into year end
19 October 2026
Key points
• September CPI to rise 0.62%mth 
with the annual pace lifting to 4.1%yr 
from 4.0%yr. The seasonally adjusted 
CPI lifts 0.5%mth, a step down from 
the 0.7% increase in August.  
• September Trimmed Mean (TM) is 
set to lift 0.26%mth holding the 
annual pace steady at 3.6%yr. 
• As we noted in our review of the 
August CPI, our September quarter 
CPI estimate is unchanged at 1.3%qtr 
while the TM remained 0.9%qtr.  
• Auto fuel surged ahead of our 
expectations so along with signs of 
increase pass-through we lifted our 
December quarter CPI to 1.3%qtr/3.9%yr 
and the TM estimate to 0.9%qtr/3.5%yr. 
• This is a 0.8ppt increase to the CPI 
estimate, and a 0.2ppt increase to the 
TM estimate, highlighting inflation 
momentum meaningfully above 
the RBA’s current expectations.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
August inflation came in soft...
Inflation moderated in August following July’s strong 
outcome. The CPI rose 0.4% in the month, in line with our 
expectation but slightly below market consensus of 0.5%, 
taking the annual rate to 4.0%. On a seasonally adjusted 
basis, the CPI increased 0.7%mth, underscoring the impact of 
seasonal discounting in August and the start of the financial 
year repricing in July.
More closely watched are the measures of underlying inflation 
including the Trimmed Mean (TM) and market services. 
The TM rose 0.2% in August, in line with our expectations 
but again softer than the market, leaving the annual pace 
unchanged at 3.6% for a third consecutive month. Meanwhile, 
market services excluding volatiles & holiday travel increased 
0.2%mth/4.1%yr. Notable strength (three-month annualised, 
seasonally adjusted) was seen in sports participation (10.9%), 
clothing repair (7.0%), restaurant meals (6.8%) and other 
household services (5.2%). For full analysis of the August CPI, 
see “August CPI – Came in on our expectations ”.
The expenditure-weighted share of the CPI basket 
experiencing positive price shocks eased to 11% in August 
from 15% in July, while the share experiencing negative price 
shocks declined from 17% to just below 13%. The majority of 
the positive shocks were concentrated in consumer durables 
On a three-month moving average basis, the net momentum 
share (positive minus negative) rose to –4.5% from –7.7% 
in July and a trough of –15.8% in May, suggesting the 
disinflationary impulse may be nearing exhaustion. 
... with September due for a moderate pick-up
Looking ahead to September, early indicators painted a 
mixed picture. The NAB business survey showed retail price 
growth slowing to 0.9%qtr from 1.1%qtr in August, with 
labour cost growth easing to 1.7%qtr from 1.8%qtr, while 
purchase cost growth edged up to 2.4%qtr from 2.3%qtr. 
Nevertheless, all measures remain well above their long-run 
averages while the gap between purchase costs and final 
prices continues to widen, indicating businesses are facing 
margin compression (see here for further detail). Meanwhile, 
consumers’ year-ahead inflation expectations held steady 
at 4.9% in September (easing slightly at 2dp) but surged to 
5.3% in October likely reflecting higher fuel prices, with retail 
petrol and diesel averaging $2.20/l (+8.2%mth) and $2.68/l 
(+7.7%mth). We do believe the auto fuel prices are likely to see 
a near term peak in October.  Meanwhile, global shipping costs 
have also surged recently amid renewed Middle East tensions.
Sep quarter unrevised but Dec upgraded significantly
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Neha Sharma, Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The September CPI is expected to rise 0.6% (0.62% to 2dp)
with the annual pace lifting to 4.1%yr. Our seasonally adjusted
estimate increases 0.5% (0.48% to 2dp). For the monthly
TM, we expect a 0.3% (0.26% to 2dp) lift, holding the annual

pace steady at 3.6%. On a 3m/3m basis, the TM is tracking
a 1.0% gain in September (noting that while this measure is
conceptually different to the quarterly TM it still serves as a
useful guide). For market services excluding volatiles & holiday
travel we have pencilled in another 0.2% rise, slowing the
annual pace slightly to 4.0% (a nine-month low).
The largest contributors are recreation & culture (+0.27ppt),
driven wholly by holiday travel & accommodation. Transport

(+0.23ppt) makes a solid contribution, with gains in fuel
(+0.26ppt) partly offset by motor vehicles (–0.02ppt). Housing
also contributes (+0.13ppt), with rents and new dwellings
each adding 0.02ppt while property rates add 0.08ppt.
The key detractors in the month are food & non-alcoholic
beverages (–0.08ppt) and garments (–0.04ppt).
Key details for the month of September
Food & non-alcoholic beverages are expected to fall
0.5%mth, easing the annual pace to 2.8%yr. The weakness is

concentrated in a 3.7%mth decline in fruit & vegetables, while
non-alcoholic beverages are also expected to fall 1.5%mth.
Meals out & takeaway is forecast to rise 0.1%mth, easing the
annual rate to 3.9%.
Housing is expected to rise 0.6% in September, slowing the
annual pace to 5.5%yr. Component-wise:
• Rents are expected to rise 0.3%mth and 3.7%yr. CPI rent
inflation is nearing its peak, consistent with the moderation
in asking rent growth. We expect rent inflation to ease to

3.6%yr by end-2026 and 2.5%yr by end-2027.
• New dwelling prices, which capture only the building
component of construction costs, are expected to rise
0.2%mth and 4.9%yr. Ongoing weakness in established
housing markets would be increasing builders’ difficulty in
passing through higher costs to new builds. As a result, we
expect new dwelling inflation to moderate to 4.5%yr by
end-2026 and 2.1%yr by end-2027.
Transport inflation is expected to rise 2.0%mth in September,

lifting the annual pace to 7.7%yr reflecting an 8.3%mth
increase in fuel prices following higher oil prices through the
month. Motor vehicle prices and spare parts are expected to
fall 0.7%mth and 1.3%mth, respectively. In NSW, a one-year
vehicle registration discount between $80-100 came into
effect from 1 September, which is likely to weigh on the other
services in respect of motor vehicles component.
Recreation & culture is expected to rise 2.1%mth driven by a

4.4%mth increase in holiday travel, partly offset by a 0.4%
decline in audio, visual & computing equipment & services and
a 1.3% fall in sporting services.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Risks to September baseline
We view the risks around the September CPI outcome as broadly 
balanced. Waning spending momentum outside of fuel may make 
it difficult for businesses to pass through higher costs in the near 
term. However, some of these pricing pressures may be deferred 
rather than forgone. The October ban on card surcharging may 
prompt a greater-than-usual degree of price resetting, allowing 
businesses to recover some of the margin pressures that have built 
up in recent months.
Risks to housing are mixed. Trade materials suppliers continued 
to announce above-average price increases through September 
(around 8%). While soft conditions in the established housing 
market may limit the extent to which these costs are passed 
through to new dwelling prices, they nevertheless present an 
upside risk to housing-related inflation, particularly maintenance 
& repair and insurance costs. Growth in advertised rents also 
continues to ease but these new leases take some time to flow 
through to average rents paid measured in the CPI. 
A kick up in October ...
We have a solid preliminary estimate for the October CPI of 
0.6%mth lifting the annual pace to 4.8%yr. It is true that the key 
driver is an 8.9% increase in auto fuel but we estimate the TM to 
lift 0.4%mth/3.7%yr highlighting broader inflationary pressures. 
We expect that the removal of credit charge surcharges and 
resulting increase in retail prices, at most, will contribute 0.1ppt 
but only just so see it as more of a rounding error. Nevertheless, 
these changes come at a time of a significant upstream price 
pressure, particularly for SMEs, so there is some risk of broader 
price adjustments. As such, we expect marginally larger than 
usual price adjustments in October particularly in hospitality 
and household services. Being the first month of the quarter the 
October lift sets a higher base for the quarterly average estimates 
resulting in an upgrade to our December quarter estimate.
We have included a table with a monthly profile for the CPI, 
seasonally adjusted CPI and the TM out to June 2027. At 
June 2027 we have an annual pace of 3.7%yr for the CPI and 
seasonally adjusted CPI and 3.3%yr for the TM. There is also a 
table breaking down the monthly components of the CPI through 
the December quarter. 
... highlights stronger inflationary pressure ...
Our September quarter CPI estimate is unrevised at 1.3%qtr 
with the annual pace steady at 3.9%yr. At the margin there 
was a 0.04ppt increase due to a lift in auto fuel but this is 
1.29%qtr at two decimal places so still rounds to 1.3%. 
The seasonally adjusted CPI gains 1.1% in the September 
quarter highlighting that part of the increase is due to usual 
seasonality. Our TM estimate for Q3 has a small downward 
revision of 0.03ppt to 0.88% at two decimal place so our 
published estimate is also unrevised at 0.9%qtr. 
... resulting in an upward revision to Q4 2026
Westpac’s December quarter CPI estimate has been boosted 
from a 0.5%qtr to 1.3%qtr. Driving most of this change from 
our previous estimate is the 15.3% increase in autofuel, it

was 1.9%, contributing 0.55ppt to the 0.8ppt increase in the 
quarterly estimate. Outside of a 0.09ppt contribution to the 
revision from holiday travel & accommodation (revised up from 
4.2% to 5.6%) all else was via small contributions for a large 
number of expenditure categories. This is why there is a 0.2ppt 
increase in our September quarter TM estimate from 0.7%qtr 
to 0.9%qtr.
A disinflationary impulse kicks in for 2027
We now expect auto fuel prices to peak in October (240 cpl 
for petrol, 284 cpl for diesel) and then to trend lower through 
2027 (184 cpl for petrol and 194 cpl for diesel by December 
2027) leading to a 13.6% falling in auto fuel in the first half 
of 2027 subtracting 0.46ppt off the CPI over the same period 
of time. For a detailed description on how we constructed the 
auto fuels profile please see: “ Persistent energy pressures 
have further to run ” 
The first half of 2027 will also see an almost 5% decline in 
holiday travel & accommodation which will subtract a further 
0.29ppt off the CPI. However, outside of that any broader 
moderation in inflation is expected to be more muted hence 
we have the TM printing 0.7%qtr for each quarter in 2027.  
Critical to this moderation in core inflation is our expect step 
down in dwelling price inflation on the back of falling estab -
lished home prices. From a 1.1%qtr increase in Q3 2026 we 
see dwelling price increasing by 0.7% in the Q4 2026, and 
then 0.6% in both Q1 and Q2 2027 before dropping to 0.5%qtr 
in Q3 and Q4 2027. This will see dwelling price inflation in the 
CPI drop from 5.4%yr at June 2026 to 3.0%yr by June 2027 
then down to 2.2%yr by December 2027. 
At least for the near term a key uncertainty remains the extent 
to which businesses are able to pass through ongoing labour 
and non-labour cost pressures and how this unwinds through 
2027 as energy prices ease and the domestic economy slows 
under the weight of higher interest rates and falling estab -
lished home prices. There is a risk that firms face greater 
resistance to price increases if discretionary household spend -
ing softens by more than expected. That said, these headwinds 
may be at least partially offset by the stronger starting point 
for household incomes, which has proven more resilient than 
previously anticipated, and by higher energy prices due to the 
ongoing conflict in the Middle East.
That said, there is also the risk that the disinflationary pulse by 
the second half of 2027 could be greater than what we antici-

pate. Another rate hike in November, along with a deeper than 
expected fall in established home prices, could be the prover -
bial “straw” resulting in a larger than expected fall in demand 
and thus a more significant moderation in price setting behav -
iour. It is also possible that our auto fuel prices fall by more 
than we anticipate. For while we have spot crude prices to fall 
to about pre-crisis levels by end 2027, a combination of a slow 
recovery in refining capacity, constrained return of physical 
supplies and higher shipping cost keep Australian fuel prices 
above their pre-conflict level. Should all these factors improve 
fast than we expect, and auto fuel prices return to pre-conflict 
level if not lower, then headline inflation could dip below 2%yr 
and the TM would be closer to 2.5%yr (the mid point if the 
RBA’s inflation target), by end 2027.

## Document 511: 【產業聚焦】降息前的美國汽車產業投資布局

Source PDF: all report pdf/【產業聚焦】降息前的美國汽車產業投資布局.pdf
Report source: CTBC
Date: 2026-10-30
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

源，但對其完整性、即時性和正確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行並不負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資

訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不涉及特定投資標的之建議。
產業聚焦降息前的美國汽車產業投資布局
2026/10/30
• 美國傳統車商迎來皮卡改款
週期
• 高油價使油電、電動車需求
提升
• 自駕車、軟體營收增加帶動
車商獲利
美國傳統車商迎來皮卡改款週期，獲利表現有望提升
美國傳統車廠近年轉向生產更多高價卡車和SUV，因部分SUV和皮卡的調整後息稅前利潤率高達20%、超
過其他車型，也使美國新車平均交易價格在9月超過5萬美元。福特與通用汽車即將在2026年年底至2027
年迎來全尺寸皮卡改款與更新計畫，由於在推出新款或新一代卡車後，通常會有2~3年較佳的銷售期，預
估福特和通用汽車在2027年及之後的獲利有機會持續提升。
美國政策轉為支持燃油車發展，但高油價使油電、電動車需求提升

儘管美國政策轉向支持燃油車發展 ，但自美伊戰爭後油價居高不下 ，使油電混合車3Q26在美國的市佔率
暴衝至16%，包括豐田 、現代等品牌在美國的市占率持續上升 。美國3Q26電動車銷量約佔新車總銷量
6.5%，高於2Q26的5.8%，顯示電動車在美國市場正重新站穩腳步 。由於油價上升使全球對電動車需求
增加，特斯拉2Q26、3Q26交車量也優於市場預期。
自駕車接受度提升，軟體營收增加帶動車商獲利
美國消費者對自動駕駛的認識與接受度正持續提升，而各車商正積極擴大自駕與軟體訂閱服務布局。通用
汽車預估其OnStar和Super Cruise自駕輔助營收在2026年將突破30億美元，福特汽車的軟體付費訂閱用
戶已達160萬戶。3Q26特斯拉FSD活躍用戶數已達174萬、年增67%，且北美新車FSD採用率超過75%。
相較傳統車商低於10%的硬體銷售利潤率，軟體毛利率高達50~70%，且訂閱制使車商部分營收轉為持續
性收入，將帶動其估值表現。
美國輕型車年化銷售量與年增率
14.9

美國9月份輕型車年化銷售量約為
1,600萬輛，低於市場預估共識的
1,630萬輛 。儘管9月初的勞動節
促銷活動使購車回饋及零售銷售
增加，但同時銷售量年減 4%也顯
示市場需求正面臨更大挑戰。
今年上半年儘管面臨高利率 、高
車價等負面影響 ，但高收入族群
支撐使美國車市仍具韌性 。然而
隨著利率上升 、對消費者可負擔
性的懷疑日益增加 ，市場目前正
逐步將2026年美國輕型車銷售量
預估由 1,610萬輛下修至 1,560至
1,600萬輛區間。
聯準會在 9月升息一碼 ，使美國
目標利率區間上升至 3.75~4%，
且目前 市場普遍預期聯準會在今
年12月及2027年上半年將再各升
息一碼 。美國約有 75~85%新車
購買使用貸款或融資 ，利率持續
上升將直接推高車貸利率 ，進而
增加消費者的購車成本與負債壓
力，且利率上升也會造成核貸門
檻轉趨嚴格 ，使消費者對新車的

需求下降。
未來若油價 、通膨下降 ，市場重
新步入降息循環後有望使美國車
市需求重新回升。資料來源：Cox Automotive，2026/9/24，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/22

福特、通用汽車全尺寸皮卡在改款當年及之後
兩年銷售表現優於同業
資料來源：Goldman Sachs，2026/10/13，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/22
車商將迎來皮卡改款週期
回饋優惠在交易價中佔比美國新車交易價格美國新車平均交易價格( ATP )在
9月超過5萬美元，車價上漲在每

年下半年為常見現象 ，以因應車
款更新而重新定價 。然而 ，9月
ATP年增率低於1%遠低於典型的
9月增幅2~4%，顯示利率上升後
平價車款銷售相對強勁。
儘管美國新車售價成長幅度趨緩 ，
但其上升趨勢仍使美國傳統車商
利潤率逐步改善 。通用、福特汽
車等傳統車廠近年 轉向生產更多
卡車和 SUV，因部分 SUV和皮卡
的調整後息稅前利潤率超過 20%，
使得車廠利潤率能維持一定水準 。
(美元)
福特與通用汽車即將在 2026年年
底至2027年迎來全尺寸皮卡改款
與 更 新 計 畫， 福 特 旗 艦 皮 卡F-
150將換上全新3.0升V6引擎，通
用汽車則將對 GMC Sierra 及
Chevrolet Silverado全尺寸皮卡
進行大改款。
鑑於福特與通用汽車在皮卡銷售
中取得的較高利潤 ，皮卡市場對
獲利的重要性可想而知 。由於原
廠在推出新款或新一代卡車後 ，
通常會有 2~3年較佳的銷售期 ，
預估福特和通用汽車在 2027年及
之後的獲利有機會持續提升。

4
高油價使美國電動車銷售逐步回溫
資料來源：Cox Automotive，2026/10/12，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/28
需求使油電、電動車並存
康寧儘管政策轉向支持燃油車發展 ，
但自美伊戰爭後油價居高不下 ，
使油電混合車3Q26在美國的市佔
率暴衝至 16%，包括豐田 、現代
等品牌在美國的市占率持續上升 。

而儘管美國3Q26電動車銷量較去
年同期下降 (3Q25為電動車補助
期限 ，基期較高 )，但仍較 2Q26
成長近8%，約佔美國新車總銷量
的6.5%，高於2Q26的5.8%，顯
示電動車在美國市場正重新站穩
腳步。以市場反應來看 ，美國車
市正從先前單一電動化路線 ，轉
向燃油 、油電、電動車並存局面 。
美國國會在 2025年9/30 正式取
消購買或租賃新電動車的 7,500
美元稅收抵免 ，造成美國電動車
銷售在 4Q25開始大幅下滑 。另
外，川普政府在今年 9月底正式
宣布放寬美國企業燃油效率標準 ，
將未來新車的燃油效率年均提升
目標下調 ，使部分車商開始降低
電動車生產以減少虧損。
傳統車企如福特 、通用汽車的電
動車部門 EBIT利潤率可能介於 -
10~-30%以下，燃油效率標準放
寬使其生產方向轉回燃油車發展 ，
也有助於改善整體獲利結構。

5
通用汽車遞延收入上升，顯示未來軟體營收增加
資料來源：公司，2026/10，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/29
軟體營收增加帶動獲利表現
各車商正積極擴大自駕與軟體訂閱
服務布局，並將其視為未來重要獲
利成長動能 。 通 用 汽 車 預 估 其
OnStar和Super Cruise自駕輔助營

收在 2026年將突破 30億美元 ，並
在未來維持雙位數成長 。福特汽車
的軟體付費訂閱用戶已達 160萬戶。
3Q26特斯拉 FSD活躍用戶數已達
174 萬 、 年增 67% ， 且 北 美 新 車
FSD採用率超過75%。
相較傳統車商低於 10%的硬體銷售
利潤率，軟體毛利率高達 50~70%，
且訂閱制使車商部分營收轉為持續
性收入，將帶動其估值表現。
美國消費者對自動駕駛的認識與接
受度正持續提升 。過去十年來 ，對
自駕車有較多了解的民眾比例由
40%增至58%，且近半數消費者相
信在有生之年將能看到自駕車普及 。
另外，消費者雖然支持車輛自動化
發展，但仍傾向保有人工接管能力 ，
因此對 Level 4 的 興 趣 最 高， 對
Level 5的興趣反而下降。
調查也發現，多數曾搭乘Robotaxi
的使用者對自動駕駛的觀感明顯改
善，未來隨著服務覆蓋範圍持續擴
大，Robotaxi有望進一步提升大眾
對自駕技術的信心與接受度。 資料來源：Cox Automotive，2026/9/28，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/29

## Document 519: ここが知りたい『投資信託販売チャネルの変化と現在地』

Source PDF: all report pdf/ここが知りたい『投資信託販売チャネルの変化と現在地』.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 1
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

投資信託販売チャネルの変化と現在地

資産運用調査部　主席研究員　大木 剛（おおき つよし）
　個人の資産運用において、 投資信託はNISAによる資
産形成やリタイアメン ト資金の運用などで、 重要性を増し
ている。 投資信託の販売では、 1998年12月の銀行窓販
解禁から四半世紀以上が経過した。 ここでは、 1999年以
降の公募投資信託の販売チャネル別の純資産額 （販売 ・
解約 ・ 時価変動が反映） と構成比率の推移を資料 １ で確認
したい。
　銀行窓販の解禁から1年後の1999年末時点で、 証券
は46.4兆円 （比率90％） 、 銀行等は2.8兆円 （同5％） とな
り、 その後も銀行等は預金取引等を背景とする顧客基盤
を活かし、 2007年末には34.0兆円 （同43％） まで比率
を高めた。 一方、 証券は45.3兆円 （同57％） と比率が低
下した。
　もっ とも、 銀行等の比率は2007～2008年をピークに
低下し、 証券の比率は上昇に転じた。 証券の純資産額に
は機関投資家も保有するETF （上場投資信託） が含まれ、
2010年代以降は日本銀行による大量買入れも統計上の
証券比率を大きく押し上げた。 資料 １ ・ ２ では、 個人向け投
資信託の動きをみるためETFを除いており、 それでも
2026年8月末時点では、 証券は159.9兆円 （同70％） 、 銀
行等は67.2兆円 （同29％） となっている。
投資信託の銀行窓販は証券にどれだけ迫ったか
　2009年以降の証券の比率上昇には、 証券各社の取組
みに加え、 いくつかの要因が考えられる。
　まず、 近年はNISAなどを活用した投資信託積立の拡大
により、 資産形成層を中心に大手オンライン証券会社の
利用が大きく増えている。
　また、 メガバンク等では、 顧客層に応じたグループ内の
銀行と証券の役割分担を強化し、 銀行顧客の富裕層や資
産形成層にもニーズに応じて、 証券がサービスを提供す
る動きが寄与している。
　さらに地域銀行において、 銀行の個人向け投資信託事
業を大手証券等との包括提携の下で共同運営する動き
も、 証券比率の上昇に作用している。
　一方で、 銀行等の純資産額も近年は堅調に伸びてお
り、 比率低下は、 銀行の落ち込みによるものではなく、 証
券の伸びが上回った影響が大きい。
　もっ とも、 「銀行と証券のどちらが高成長か」 という比較
より、 顧客ニーズに応じた役割分担の重要性が増してい
る。 2026年7月に政府が策定した 「金融戦略」 も、 インベ
ス トメン トチェーンの機能強化を掲げている。 多様な資産
運用ニーズに各金融サービスの担い手が強みを活かし
て応えることが、 資産運用立国の深化につながろう。
資産運用立国の深化に求められる取組み
資料1 公募投資信託 販売チャネル別純資産額推移

中国
(株)日本総合研究所 中国経済展望 2026年9月2
景気は内需主因で停滞
◆消費・投資はコロナ禍以来の低い伸び
中国景気は内需の不振で足踏み 。小売売上高
は、財消費の低迷を主因に前年並みの水準で推移。
耐久消費財の買い替え補助金の効果がはく落して
いるほか、中東危機によるガソリン価格の高騰などを
背景に消費者マインドが一段と悪化 。固定資産投
資はインフラ投資などで底打ちの兆しがみられるもの
の、昨年以降大幅な減少が継続。
一方、外需は高い伸びを維持 。世界的なAIブー
ムを背景にIT関連財の輸出が引き続き好調である
ほか、EV需要の高まりを受けて自動車輸出台数も
3カ月連続で過去最高水準。もっとも、原油や半導
体などの価格上昇の影響も大きく 、実質ベースでは、
内需の減速を補うには至らず。
◆成長率目標の達成は困難
先行きの景気も、内需を中心に停滞が続く見通し。
ハイテク製造業の投資拡大や電力 ・通信網などの
「６つのネットワーク 」推進によるインフラ投資の持ち
直しが見込まれるものの 、不動産不況や過剰生産

能力といった構造的な下押し要因が残存し 、投資
は総じて低調に推移する見込み。悲観的な消費者
マインドが続くなか、消費も低迷が続く公算大。
一方、政府は景気刺激策に慎重 。成長率よりも
経済の質を重視し 、不動産市場や地方政府債務
などのリスク解消に注力する姿勢を明確にしており 、
2026年の成長率目標は達成困難な状況。
経済政策スタンス（鐘才文コラム）
（資料）人民網などを基に日本総研作成
（注）中央財経委員会が「鐘才文」名義で掲載した文章。
現状認識
・AI、ロボット、ハイテク製造業など新たな成長ドラ
イバーが台頭
・成長モデルの転換期にあり、成長率や月次指標
よりも経済運営の質を重視
・供給は強い一方、需要が弱い状況は残存
経済運営の
方向性
・AIや先端製造業を軸とした産業高度化を推進
・大規模な景気刺激策には依存しない
・不動産市場や地方政府債務などのリスク解消策
に注力（短期の成長率にはマイナスとなるも、長
期かつ持続的な経済発展にはプラス）
下半期の
経済見通し
・第15次５カ年計画などで確定した重点プロジェ

クトを執行すれば、経済は安定的に推移

(株)日本総合研究所 中国経済展望 2026年9月3
消費は総じて低調
◆サービス消費は堅調も、財消費が低迷
８月の小売売上高は前年比＋ 0.3％と、ほぼゼ
ロ成長。財消費の低迷が主因であり、なかでも自動
車が大幅減。買い替え補助政策で喚起された需要
の反動減に加え、原油高でガソリン車需要が減退。
宝飾品も 、国際的な金価格の上昇と節約志向の
高まりが相まって、販売数量が大幅減。
先行きの消費も、総じて低調に推移する見通し。
消費者マインドは中東危機を契機に悪化基調 。６
月に一時持ち直したものの 、ガソリン価格の再上昇
を受けて再び低下 。政府は利子補給制度の対象
拡大などで内需を喚起しているものの 、大規模な消
費刺激策は打ち出さない姿勢。一方、サービス消費
は今後の消費拡大の重点分野に位置づけられてお
り、堅調に推移する見込み。
◆住宅市場は長期停滞から脱却できず
住宅市場は引き続き低迷。大都市を中心に住宅
購入要件の緩和などが実施されているものの 、購入
層の減少といった構造的な要因が住宅販売の重石

に。新築住宅価格に加え 、市場の実勢を反映しや
すい中古住宅価格も引き続き下落。在庫の回転期
間も長期化している模様。住宅市場の健全化に向
け、予約販売から完成後販売への移行や住宅ロー
ン返済期間の延長などが打ち出されたものの 、効果
が短期間で現れるとは見込めず 、住宅市場の本格
回復にはなお数年を要する見通し。
住宅販売に関する新たな政策

### Page 5

中国
(株)日本総合研究所 中国経済展望 2026年9月4
◆投資は下げ止まりの兆し
８月の固定資産投資は前年比 ▲10.6％と、大
幅減が続いているものの、一部で底打ちの兆し。セク
ター別にみると 、インフラ投資は 、「６つのネットワー
ク」の推進などを背景にマイナス幅が縮小。一方、製
造業投資は減少幅が拡大 。政府は経済運営の質
を重視する姿勢をとっており、過剰生産能力の解消
が投資の重石になっている可能性。不動産投資も、
住宅市場の低迷を背景に大幅減少が継続。
◆年末にかけてマイナス幅が縮小
先行きの投資は、以下２点から減少幅が縮小す
る見通し。第１に、ハイテク製造業の投資拡大。政
府による支援も相まって、半導体やロボットなど先端
分野は増産基調。第２に、インフラ投資の持ち直し。
8,000億元の新型政策性金融ツールと「６つのネッ
トワーク（六張網）」が年後半のインフラ投資を押し
上げ。六張網は官民が重点的にインフラ整備を進め
る戦略分野であり 、既存整備計画の前倒しのみな
らず、次世代通信網や計算能力網などの新規計画

も立ち上げ。足元では、情報伝送や鉄道輸送 、航
空輸送に関連する固定資産投資に底打ちの兆し。
もっとも、本格回復は見込めない状況 。製造業や
不動産業では、過剰な生産能力や在庫などの構造
的な下押し要因が残存 。インフラ投資も量的拡大
は限定的。財源となる地方債は借り換えや隠れ債
務の圧縮が優先され、新発債の発行は停滞。
投資は下げ止まるも、弱い動き

### Page 6

中国
(株)日本総合研究所 中国経済展望 2026年9月5
外需は先行きも堅調
◆輸出入ともに価格上昇の影響大
８月の名目輸出は前年比＋ 24.9％と、高い伸
び。ASEAN・NIEs向けでは、IT関連財の輸出が引
き続き好調であるほか 、中東危機による原油供給
懸念を背景にEV輸出も拡大。加えて、対米輸出が
米中関係の改善を受けて堅調に推移しているほか、
EU向け輸出も、年初ほどの勢いはないものの底堅く
推移。こうした輸出額の増加には 、価格上昇の影
響も大。原油価格が高止まりしているほか 、AIブー
ムを背景に半導体や銅 、金属鉱石などの価格も上
昇。実質ベースの輸出の伸びは弱く、内需の減速を
補うには至らず。
先行きも、外需は堅調に推移する見通し。９月の
米中首脳会談でも貿易摩擦の緩和に向けた認識
で一致しており、対米輸出は前年比プラスで推移す
る見通し。また、中東危機を受けて世界的な脱炭
素化が一段と加速しており 、EV輸出が拡大する公
算大。ASEANなどへの中間財輸出も 、AIブームを

背景に好調を維持する見込み。
◆資金流出は拡大傾向
金融収支は流出傾向が継続 。内需の不振で対
内直接投資が減少しているほか 、準備資産も縮小。
商業銀行などへの外貨資金の振替・預託や資産再
配分が進行している可能性 。先行きも、内需の低
迷が対内投資の重石となり 、金融収支でみた資本
流出傾向が継続する見通し。

### Page 7

中国
(株)日本総合研究所 中国経済展望 2026年9月6
◆根強いディスインフレ圧力
８月の生産者物価（PPI）は前年比＋3.8％と、
高水準で推移。原油価格に起因する生産財価格
の高止まりが主因 。消費者物価（CPI）も、総合
ベースで同＋0.8％、コアベースで同＋1.0％と、前
月からやや加速。交通燃料のプラス幅が再び拡大し
たほか、半導体の価格高騰で通信機器が上昇 。
もっとも、インフレ率は政府目標の２ ％前後を大きく
下回っており、内需の停滞に起因するディスインフレ
圧力が強い状況。
先行きも、エネルギー価格の高止まりや半導体価
格の上昇を背景に一部財の価格上昇圧力は根強
いものの 、内需の低迷により基調的なインフレ率は
低位で推移する見込み。
◆対米ドルレートは増価基調
９月の人民元の対米ドルレートは増価基調で推
移。中東危機が経済に与える影響や金融政策の
動向を見極めようと、市場の様子見姿勢が続くもの
の、好調な輸出や資本流出の抑制に向けた人民銀
行の元高容認姿勢もあり、小幅な人民元高圧力が

続く見通し。
◆株価は下落基調
９月の上海総合株価指数は下落。PPIの上昇率
が一服したことを好感して持ち直す動きがみられるも
のの、中東情勢の不透明感や内需の弱さが相場の
重石に。景気が停滞局面に入る なか、先行きも弱
い動きが続く見込み。
インフレ率は低位で推移

米中対立の常態化や米国の自国第一主義の強まりを背景に、既存の国際経済秩序が揺らいでいます。こうしたなか、各国は存
在感を高めるグローバルサウス諸国との外交・経済関係の強化を急いでいます。日本も、OECD（経済協力開発機構）などと協調し
ながら、関係強化を目指していますが、現状はASEANやインドなどに偏重しており、アフリカや中南米との連携は限定的です。本シン
ポジウムでは、はじめに世界経済の構造変化や日本のグローバルサウス諸国との連携策の現状・課題を多角的に検証します。その
上で、日本がグローバルサウス諸国との関係を深化させるための具体的な戦略を議論します。

事件  1
10/28 SK 海⼒⼠第三季營業利益 83.3 兆韓元，營益率 80% 創新⾼
五⼤重點
1  SK 海⼒⼠ 10/28 公布第三季營收 104.6 兆韓元，季增 32% 、年增 328% ，⾸度單季突破 100 兆韓
元；營業利益 83.3 兆韓元，季增 38% 、年增 632% ，營益率 80% ，較前季 76% 上升 3 個百分點。
2   營收與營業利益分別⾼於市場預估的 98.2 兆與 77.0 兆韓元，約超出 6.5% 與 8.3% ；淨利 39.8 兆

韓元，季減 58% ，期末淨現⾦ 98.7 兆韓元，較前季增加。

### Page 2

3   公司說明成⻑來⾃ HBM4 正式出貨與伺服器 DDR5 銷售增加， NAND 則由 321 層企業級 SSD 帶
動；預期第四季 HBM4 出貨量與營收將超越 HBM3E ，成為主流產品。
4   公司已與 14 家主要客戶（含全球雲端業者）簽訂多為 5 年期的⻑約，涵蓋 2027 ⾄ 2028 年逾 50%
產能；價格在客戶議定區間內反映市況，公司預期記憶體供給短缺延續⾄ 2030 年。
5  2026 年股東回饋⽬標約 100 兆韓元，含 30 兆以上現⾦股利與 60 兆以上庫藏股；第三季每股配息
2 萬韓元（含 1 萬韓元鎧俠投資收益特別股息），並通過 20 兆韓元庫藏股。
三⼤結論
1   營益率 80% 與⻑約覆蓋逾半產能，確認 HBM 與伺服器記憶體仍處供給受限、價格可轉嫁的階
段，⽀持記憶體族群維持在 AI 供應鏈中⾼於⼀般組裝的評分。
2   ⻑約只鎖定產能⽐例，價格仍隨市況在區間內調整，產能預訂不等於交貨與回款；⾼⽑利也會
提⾼後續擴產誘因，不能把單季營益率線性外推。

3   當後續季度 ASP 、位元出貨與營業現⾦流持續同步改善、庫存天數未再上升時，應分批提⾼記
憶體衛星部位；若報價回落或應收帳款增加快於營收，應先降低⾼ beta 記憶體與 KOSPI 槓桿部
位。
事件  2
10/27 以⾊列國會⼤選出⼝⺠調：內塔尼亞胡陣營僅 50 席未過半
五⼤重點
1   以⾊列第 26 屆國會選舉 10/27 晚間結束投票，第 12 與第 13 頻道出⼝⺠調皆顯⽰內塔尼亞胡陣營
約 50 席，反對派陣營 52 ⾄ 53 席，兩者均未達組閣所需的 61 席過半⾨檻。
2   阿拉伯政黨預估取得 17 ⾄ 18 席，成為關鍵少數；利庫德集團以 24 ⾄ 25 席仍是國會第⼀⼤黨，
領先前參謀總⻑艾森科特領導的政黨。
3   投票率⾄晚間 8 時達 74.3% ，為 1988 年以來同時段最⾼；內塔尼亞胡陣營席次較 2022 年的 64 席
減少約 14 席（推算），反對派領袖拉⽪德稱其「全⾯崩潰」。
4   中央選舉委員會表⽰逾 65 萬張雙信封選票需另⾏計票，正式結果可能要到本週五才確定，之後

由總統赫佐格諮詢各黨，再指定組閣⼈選。
5   國家團結黨主席⽢茨呼籲跨陣營組成團結政府以避免再次⼤選；極右派閣員本格維爾則反對與
艾森科特合組政府，組閣僵局是主要⾵險。

### Page 3

三⼤結論
1   以⾊列在中東衝突期間出現政權更替可能，短期內政策⾛向與停戰談判⽴場的不確定性上升，
地緣溢價仍是油價與避險資產的核⼼變數。
2   選舉結果需數⽇確認、組閣談判可能拉⻑，期間政策空窗使中東情勢難以在短期內明朗，原油
宜維持事件避險定位，不把單⼀政治事件外推為⻑期供給變化。
3   當新政府組成並明確推動停戰談判、油價回落時，應逐步降低原油與能源股的事件避險部位；
若組閣失敗⾛向再次⼤選或戰事升溫，則保留⿈⾦與短券作為尾端⾵險分散。
事件  3
10/27 伊朗最⾼領袖要求落實停戰條件， WTI 原油收 99.90 美元跌破百元
五⼤重點
1   紐約 12 ⽉輕原油 10/27 收每桶 99.90 美元，下跌 1.86 美元、跌幅 1.83% ，收在百元以下；倫敦布
蘭特 12 ⽉期貨收 113.88 美元，下跌 1.93 美元、跌幅 1.67% 。
2   伊朗最⾼領袖穆傑塔巴 · 哈梅內伊 10/27 會⾒最⾼國家安全委員會秘書時表⽰，應落實伊朗官員
提出的條件，以結束戰爭與「既⾮戰爭、也⾮和平」的局⾯。

3   同⼀會談也討論透過管控荷莫茲海峽維護區域安全，顯⽰伊朗仍以海峽作為談判籌碼；美國總
統川普則稱若重來仍會攻擊伊朗，談判前景未定。
4   外交斡旋同步進⾏：伊朗、沙烏地、⼟⽿其與巴基斯坦 10/26 同意持續磋商區域安全，普丁
10/27 在莫斯科會晤伊朗總統與卡達元⾸，市場因此下修供給中斷⾵險。
5   美國⽯油協會（ API ）公布截⾄ 10/23 當週原油庫存減少 203.1 萬桶，與預期增加 52.2 萬桶相
反，顯⽰實體供需仍偏緊，油價下⾏空間受庫存限制。
三⼤結論
1   布蘭特仍在每桶 110 美元以上，遠⾼於第四季每桶 105 美元的情境均價假設，能源型通膨對美
國 CPI 與企業成本的壓⼒尚未解除。
2   外交訊號使油價⾃⾼點回落，但伊朗把停戰與⾃⾝條件、海峽管控綁在⼀起，供給⾵險屬可逆
但未消除，原油與能源股仍應定位為事件避險⽽⾮⻑期基準。
3   當航運恢復、 WTI 穩定在每桶 100 美元以下且庫存回升時，應逐步對能源股獲利了結；若油價
再突破前⾼並帶動核⼼服務價格上⾏，應降低⻑久期資產並保留短券。

### Page 4

事件  4
10/27 美國 10 ⽉諮商會消費者信⼼指數升⾄ 91.7 ，四個⽉來⾸度回升
五⼤重點
1   美國經濟諮商會 10/27 公布 10 ⽉消費者信⼼指數 91.7 ，較 9 ⽉上修後的 84.2 上升 7.5 點，為四個
⽉來⾸度回升，遠⾼於市場預期的 81.0 。
2   現況指數升 8.6 點⾄ 120.4 ，預期指數升 6.9 點⾄ 72.6 ；⺠眾對當前與未來企業景氣的看法同步改
善，帶動兩項分項指數⼀起回升。
3   諮商會資深經濟學家 McCormick 說明，回升主要因 9 ⽉⾄ 10 ⽉零售汽油價格回落；但他也指出
信⼼仍處⻑期下⾏趨勢，消費者計畫減少服務與耐久財⽀出，原因是價格偏⾼與通膨持續。
4   消費者未來 12 個⽉平均通膨預期回落，但仍⾼達 5.8% ；數據公布後 CME FedWatch 顯⽰，市
場預期 10/28 FOMC 維持利率不變的機率為 92.8% ，升息 1 碼機率 7.2% 。
5   諮商會調查顯⽰， 10 ⽉預期未來 12 個⽉股價上漲的消費者多於預期下跌者，股市與油價⾛勢同

時改善了家庭對資產與⽣活成本的感受。
三⼤結論
1   信⼼回升主要來⾃油價回落，⽽⾮實質所得改善；消費者同時規劃減少服務與耐久財⽀出，顯
⽰⾼物價仍在侵蝕⽀出意願，消費韌性仍需零售與就業數據確認。
2  5.8% 的通膨預期偏⾼，不利於 Fed 在本次會議釋出降息訊號；政策利率維持 3.75% ⾄ 4.00% 的
機率⾼，短券仍適合承接政策不確定性。
3   當油價維持回落、通膨預期連續下降且核⼼服務價格廣度收斂時，可把到期短券逐步轉向⾼品
質股票；若通膨預期回升並推⾼核⼼通膨，應降低遠期現⾦流占⽐⾼的估值部位。
事件  5
10/27 波⾳第三季核⼼每股虧損 0.34 美元，股價重挫 10.7%
五⼤重點
1   波⾳ 10/27 公布第三季營收 242 億美元，⾼於預期的 238 億美元；但核⼼每股虧損 0.34 美元，⽐
LSEG 統計分析師平均預期的虧損 0.21 美元更差， GAAP 每股虧損 0.26 美元。
2   ⺠⽤⾶機部⾨營收 107 億美元，營業虧損 6.97 億美元，利潤率 -6.5% ；國防部⾨盈餘近 5 億美

元，利潤率升⾄ 6.2% ，其中含出售⼦公司的 2.52 億美元收益。

### Page 5

3   公司說明第三季遭遇供應商短缺，導致⼯廠停⼯並衝擊 KC-46A 加油機與 777 貨機計畫；預期供
應⼲擾延續⾄ 2027 年，異常⽣產成本將於發⽣時認列為費⽤。
4   公司表⽰今年將無法達成交付 500 架 737 MAX 的⽬標， 737 ⽉產能由 42 架提升⾄ 47 架的時程延
後， 777-8 客機⾸架交付不會早於 2030 年。
5   股價收 168.18 美元，下跌 10.69% ，成交量約為三個⽉均量的近 7 倍；淨負債降⾄ 252 億美元，
前季為 259 億美元，資產負債表持續改善。
三⼤結論
1   營收優於預期但虧損擴⼤，代表問題在產能與成本⽽⾮需求；供應短缺延續⾄ 2027 年，⺠⽤
⾶機轉盈時程後延，⾶機訂單強勁無法直接轉成獲利。
2   供應鏈瓶頸使⼯業公司營收創⾼未必帶來⽑利改善，與交期拉⻑未必等於現⾦流改善的判斷⼀
致；美股配置仍應以⾃由現⾦流與資產負債表篩選，⽽⾮只看訂單。
3   當波⾳ 737 產能穩定提升⾄ 47 架、供應商交付恢復且⾃由現⾦流持續為正時，才適合提⾼航太

供應鏈曝險；若異常⽣產成本持續擴⼤，應避開依賴單⼀客戶交付的供應商。
事件  6
10/27 普丁稱需考慮結束衝突並解除制裁，俄烏局部停⽕⽣效
五⼤重點
1   俄羅斯總統普丁 10/27 在莫斯科天然氣出⼝國論壇峰會期間表⽰，俄羅斯需要考慮結束正在進
⾏的衝突並解除制裁，同時正探索與美國夥伴合作的可能性。
2   克⾥姆林宮助理烏沙科夫表⽰，普丁與芬蘭總統斯圖布、挪威⾸相斯特勒通話，證實澤倫斯基
可能訪問莫斯科，俄⽅將提供包括安全保障在內的必要安排，這是普丁⼀年多來⾸度與兩國領袖
通話。
3   赫爾松地區奧列什基的臨時停⽕⾃ 10/27 起⽣效、持續⾄ 11/1 ，俄烏雙⽅均未記錄違反停⽕；
烏克蘭外⻑表⽰已準備參加在阿聯舉⾏的烏美俄三⽅會談。
4   俄烏能源停⽕後，俄羅斯 10 ⽉下旬煉油廠原油加⼯量回升約 25% ；同時預期 11 ⽉波羅的海與⿊
海港⼝原油出⼝⽉減每⽇ 10 萬⾄ 20 萬桶，國內加⼯增加使出⼝減少。
5   克⾥姆林宮發⾔⼈佩斯科夫表⽰，這次通話可能影響阿布達⽐會談進程，但現在下定論尚早；

官⽅對通話結果的正式聲明仍在準備中。

### Page 6

三⼤結論
1   俄⽅釋出結束衝突與解除制裁的訊號，與俄烏局部停⽕同時出現，降低歐洲能源與柴油供給的
尾端⾵險，與油價⾃⾼點回落⽅向⼀致。
2   解除制裁與停⽕仍停在表態與局部安排，三⽅會談尚未舉⾏，俄羅斯原油出⼝反⽽預期減少，
能源供給改善需要實際貿易流量確認。
3   當三⽅會談達成更⼤範圍停⽕、柴油與原油出⼝恢復時，應降低能源股與原油的事件避險權
重；若會談破局或停⽕中斷，應維持低成本、⾼現⾦流能源股作短期通膨對沖。
事件  7
10/27 美國 700 億美元 5 年期公債標售，得標殖利率 4.845%
五⼤重點
1   美國財政部 10/27 標售 700 億美元 5 年期公債，得標殖利率 4.845% ，低於上次標售的 5.03% ；投
標倍數 2.33 ，⾼於上次的 2.21 與近期平均 2.31 。
2   得標殖利率較發⾏前交易⽔準僅⾼ 0.4 個基點，需求⼤致符合預期；按最⾼得標利率分配的⽐
例為 98.6% ，上次為 30.34% 。
3   間接投標者（多為海外官⽅與資產管理機構）占 60.0% ，低於平均 64.0% ；直接投標者占

29.4% ，⾼於平均 23.1% ，初級交易商承接 10.6% ，低於平均 13.0% 。
4  10 年期公債殖利率在 10/26 連續三週下滑後收在 5% 下⽅，市場⼀⽅⾯避險買盤增加，⼀⽅⾯預
期 10/28 FOMC 維持利率不變，降低近端升息壓⼒。
5   對照 10 ⽉中旬 10 年期公債標售投標倍數 2.77 ，為 2014 年以來⾼點，近期標售連續顯⽰⾼殖利
率吸引邊際買盤，但 5 年期海外買盤⽐重仍低於平均。
三⼤結論
1  5 年期得標殖利率由 5.03% 降⾄ 4.845% 、投標倍數回升，顯⽰⾼票息持續吸引需求，⽀持⻑債
由過度低配回到中性觀察的判斷。
2   海外間接投標⽐重低於平均，⻑端供給、財政⾚字與能源通膨仍未解決，單次標售結果不⾜以
確認期限溢價已轉為持續收斂。
3   當後續多次標售需求穩定、核⼼通膨回落且期限溢價持續下降時，應分段延⻑存續期；在此之
前，應以短天期國庫券承接流動性，⻑債只作分批試單。

### Page 7

事件  8
10/27 美國 9 ⽉耐久財訂單⽉增 1.8% ，運輸設備帶動
五⼤重點
1   美國普查局 10/27 公布 9 ⽉耐久財新訂單 3,454 億美元，⽉增 1.8% ，為五個⽉來最⼤增幅； 8 ⽉
由原先數值修正為⽉增 0.1% 。
2   運輸設備訂單⽉增 4.6% ⾄ 1,198 億美元，是主要動能；扣除運輸設備後新訂單僅⽉增 0.5% ，扣
除國防則⽉增 2.2% 。
3   ⾮國防資本財扣除⾶機（核⼼資本財）新訂單⽉增 0.5% ⾄ 880 億美元，出貨⽉增 1.2% ⾄ 861 億
美元，出貨增幅⾼於訂單，代表企業投資正在落地。
4   ⾶機訂單強勁與同⽇波⾳說明供應商短缺、交付⽬標無法達成並存，顯⽰航太需求在訂單端，
瓶頸在產能端，訂單轉成產出的速度受限。
5   扣除國防後新訂單⽉增 2.2% ，⾼於整體的 1.8% ，代表本⽉國防訂單下滑（推算），⺠間需求才
是 9 ⽉訂單的主要增量來源。
三⼤結論
1   扣除運輸後訂單僅增 0.5% ，耐久財強勁主要靠⾶機訂單，企業設備需求溫和擴張⽽⾮全⾯加

速，與擴張期但成⻑分化的景氣判斷⼀致。
2   核⼼資本財出貨⾼於訂單增速，代表 AI 與企業設備投資持續轉成實際⽀出，⽀撐美國成⻑韌
性，也使 Fed 在通膨仍⾼時缺乏降息理由。
3   當核⼼資本財訂單連續兩期轉為負成⻑、出貨同步放緩時，應降低⼯業與資本財週期部位；若
訂單與出貨持續雙增，可維持美股中⼤型現⾦流公司為核⼼。
事件  9
10/27 滙豐第三季稅前盈餘 96 億美元略低於預期，股價跌 5.9%
五⼤重點
1   滙豐 10/27 公布第三季稅前盈餘 96 億美元，年增 32% ，略低於市場預估的 97 億美元；營收 186
億美元，年增 5% ，低於預估的 189.3 億美元。
2   稅前盈餘增幅中約 14 億美元來⾃去年同期法律準備未再發⽣；扣除特殊項⽬後為 100 億美元，
年增 9 億美元，第三季有形股東權益報酬率 17.7% 。

### Page 8

3   普通股權益第⼀類資本⽐率降⾄ 13.9% ，低於市場預估的 14.1% 與公司 14% ⾄ 14.5% ⽬標區間
下緣，因⾵險性資產增加與買回庫藏股。
4   公司宣布再買回最多 10 億美元庫藏股，並維持 2026 年銀⾏淨利息收⼊⾄少 460 億美元等指引不
變；財務⻑郭珮瑛說明，股價下跌源於市場期待更⾼的業績指引。
5   倫敦股價收跌 5.91% ，⾹港股價跌逾 6% ⾄ 142 港元，為 6 ⽉以來最低；預期信⽤損失 8 億美元，
年減 17% ，⾹港商⽤不動產相關損失較去年減少。
三⼤結論
1   盈餘與營收⼩幅不及預期、資本⽐率跌破⽬標區間且指引未上修，市場對⼤型銀⾏的要求已從
獲利成⻑轉向資本與回饋能否持續擴⼤。
2   ⾹港商⽤不動產損失下降、信⽤成本年減，顯⽰資產品質未惡化；股價修正主要反映估值與指
引落差，⽽⾮信⽤⾵險擴散。
3   當滙豐資本⽐率回到 14% 以上、淨利息收⼊指引上修時，可回補資本充⾜的⼤型⾦融股；若信
⽤損失回升或資本⽐率持續下滑，應降低銀⾏股權重。

事件  10
10/27 台灣央⾏： 9 ⽉放款與投資年增 9.79% ，創 1998 年來新⾼
五⼤重點
1   台灣央⾏ 10/27 公布 9 ⽉ M2 年增 7.24% ，⾼於 8 ⽉的 6.79% ； M1B 年增 7.20% ，前 9 ⽉ M2 平均年
增 6.69% ，央⾏說明 M2 加速來⾃放款與投資成⻑及企業海外融資資⾦暫存國內。
2   全體貨幣機構放款與投資年增 9.79% ，⾼於 8 ⽉的 9.43% ，為 1998 年 12 ⽉以來新⾼；央⾏官員
表⽰ AI 帶動的資⾦需求是主要動能。
3   外匯存款餘額達 10.06 兆元，⾸度突破 10 兆元，央⾏說明主要來⾃出⼝收⼊穩定與企業海外可
轉債募資暫存，並以聯發科 39 億美元發⾏為例，台幣貶值的影響有限。
4   證券劃撥存款餘額 4.70 兆元、融資餘額 8,343 億元皆創新⾼，融資較 8 ⽉的 7,589 億元增加 754 億
元（推算），顯⽰散戶資⾦與槓桿持續流⼊股市。
5   加權指數 10/27 收 46,096 點、漲 0.12% ，接近六週低點，明顯落後同⽇ KOSPI 漲 3.09% ；央⾏下

次⾦融情況發布為 11/23 。
三⼤結論

### Page 9

1   放款與投資創 27 年新⾼、外匯存款破 10 兆元，印證 AI 出⼝收⼊與資⾦需求同時擴張，但企業
把美元留在帳上，台幣升值仍將維持平緩。
2   融資餘額創⾼⽽加權指數接近六週低點，代表槓桿資⾦在⾼檔回檔時進場，台股短線波動與追
繳⾵險上升，需區分有議價權的⼤型廠與⾼槓桿題材股。
3   當出⼝與放款持續⾼增、融資餘額增速放緩時，應維持先進製程與現⾦流核⼼；若融資持續攀
升⽽市場廣度轉弱，應降低台股⾼ beta 部位，並保留部分美元短券作匯率緩衝。

## Document 622: 凱基投顧_5289 宜鼎_沈漢軒_20261028

Source PDF: all report pdf/凱基投顧_5289 宜鼎_沈漢軒_20261028.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-28
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

十月 28, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  Powered by the EFA Platform 1

動態更新  記憶體 ‧ 台灣

宜鼎 
(5289.TWO /5289 TT )

持續深化 AI 基礎建設布局 
重要訊息 
我們針對宜鼎 3Q26 財報進行前瞻，並分析後續營運展望。 
評論及分析  
預估 3Q26 毛利率將小幅下滑。3Q26 營收 258.8 億元，季增 15.3%。我們
推估營收成長主要反映： (1)DRAM/NAND 位元出貨量大致持平；
(2)DRAM/NAND ASP 季增 10-20%；(3)安提貢獻營收 4-5 億元。受記憶體報
價漲幅收斂影響，預估毛利率季減 4.5ppts 至 60.5%，每股盈餘 117.1 元。 
AI 基礎建設比重提升 ，客戶結構 持續優化。宜鼎客戶結構近一至兩年顯著
轉變，由工控與 IPC 逐步轉向 AI 基礎建設。我們預估 後者營收比重已超過
40%，主要客戶涵蓋 Switch 大廠、GPU 大廠及美系/中系 CSP，供應產品包
括 DDR4 顆粒、DDR5 RDIMM 及企業級儲存產品( 以 TLC 為主)。展望 2027
年，我們預期上述應用營收將持續穩健成長，其中企業級儲存有望加速放
量。公司過去以 SLC/MLC 及 BiCS3/ 5 小容量 TLC 為主，今年起逐步轉向
BiCS8，多數客戶預計於 2027 年完成驗證並放量出貨，主要應用於 boot 
drive 與 eSSD。隨產品升級及客戶驗證陸續完成，預估 2027 年 AI 基礎建設
營收比重有望超過 50%，趨近整體產業平均 ，客戶結構持續優化 。  
2027 年營收穩健成長，報價漲幅收斂使獲利 微幅下滑 。我們預估安提
2026- 27 年營收分別達 21.9 億/30.4 億元，年增 169.4% /38.5%，主要動能
來自： (1)記憶體漲價帶動整機 ASP 提升； (2)兩大人型機器人客戶出貨成
長。惟記憶體本業規模快速擴大，安提營收占比已降至約 2%，後續占比提
升將取決於人型機器人能否大規模放量，以及 AI ASIC 客戶能否順利量產。
展望 2027 年，隨 AI 基礎建設營收比重提升及台系廠商加入供應鏈，宜鼎可
望維持穩定貨源。我們預估 DRAM /NAND 位元出貨量分別年增 4%/35%，
其中 NAND 主要受惠 TLC 比重提升；DRAM /NAND ASP 則分別年增
21%/18%，帶動全年營收年增 38.5%至 1,234.2 億元。惟報價漲幅趨緩，
預估毛利率回落至 43.3%，EPS 為 375.1 元，年減 5.4%。 
投資建議  
考量目前股價僅交易於 2026 年 2.7 倍本益比，評價具吸引力，凱基維持對
宜鼎正向看法，重申「增加持股」評等，目標價 1,500 元，隱含 2027 年 4
倍本益比，低於模組廠龍頭群聯的 7 倍。 
投資風險  
記憶體供貨短缺、毛利率降幅超出預期 。 
增加持股‧維持 
  收盤價 October 28 (NT$)   1,160  
12 個月目標價 (NT$) 1,500  
前次目標價  (NT$) 1,100

調升 (%) 36.4

上漲空間  (%) 29.3

焦點內容   
1. 預估 3Q26 毛利率將小幅下滑。 
2. AI 基礎建設比重提升，客戶結構持續
優化。 
3. 2027 年營收穩健成長，報價漲幅收

圖 3: 公司概況  圖 4: 記憶體模組佔宜鼎營收大宗 
宜鼎成立於 2005 年，並於 2013 年正式掛牌上櫃，為一家以
服務為導向的工業用儲存裝置供應商，生產基地位於新北汐止
及宜蘭。公司專注於企業級、工業、航太、 醫療與國防等應用
市場，提供工業用嵌入式儲存裝置與工業用動態隨機記憶體模
組與相關技術服務，並以自有品牌「 Innodisk 」行銷全球。宜
鼎結合集團旗下公司 安提、維新、安捷科、巽晨，產品涵蓋記
憶體模組到 AIoT 周邊產品，提供客戶從雲端到邊緣端，一站
式完整 AI 解決方案。
